ChatGPT said:
You’ve shared a package.json. It’s broadly coherent for a Payload v2 + TS + Express backend with a separate frontend app under src/app, but there are several practical issues and a few “likely to break later” items.

What looks fine
main: "dist/server.js" and serve running node dist/server.js are consistent.

Using PAYLOAD_CONFIG_PATH for both dev and prod is a standard pattern for Payload.

Split build (build:payload, build:server, build:app) is reasonable.

Issues / risks I would address
1) dotenv is very old
You have "dotenv": "^8.2.0". That’s years behind current versions and can cause friction with other tooling or docs/examples.

Recommendation: upgrade dotenv (and verify no breaking changes in your usage).

Typical current range is much newer than v8.

2) Payload package versions are inconsistent
You have:

"payload": "^2.0.0"

@payloadcms/plugin-cloud": "^2.0.0"

@payloadcms/bundler-webpack": "^1.0.0"

@payloadcms/db-mongodb": "^1.0.0"

@payloadcms/richtext-slate": "^1.0.0"

This mix is a common source of dependency mismatch (especially when Payload core has moved forward but plugins/bundlers are pinned to older major ranges).

Recommendation: align Payload core and all @payloadcms/* packages to compatible versions (ideally the same minor line). If this repo was created from an older template, you may be locking yourself into an early v2 ecosystem.

3) dev script may not run TS correctly depending on your nodemon config
Your dev script is:

"dev": "cross-env PAYLOAD_CONFIG_PATH=src/payload.config.ts nodemon"
This relies on nodemon being configured (via nodemon.json) to run ts-node (or a loader) and to watch .ts files. If you do not have a nodemon.json (or nodemon CLI args) set up, nodemon may try to run a default index.js and fail, or run the wrong entrypoint.

Recommendation: make the entry explicit, e.g. something along the lines of:

nodemon --exec ts-node src/server.ts
or use Node’s TS loader configuration appropriate for your setup.

(Exact command depends on your server entry file and module system.)

4) copyfiles patterns and -u 1 are easy to get wrong
This command:

"copyfiles": "copyfiles -e 'src/app/**' -u 1 'src/**/*.{html,css,scss,ttf,woff,woff2,eot,svg,jpg,png,ico}' dist/ && copyfiles -u 1 'src/app/dist/**' dist/"
Potential problems:

-u 1 strips one path segment; with nested assets you may end up with unexpected output structure in dist/.

Quoting/globbing can behave differently on Windows vs macOS/Linux depending on shell; copyfiles usually handles globs, but quoting style sometimes trips users up.

You’re excluding src/app/** from the first copy, then copying src/app/dist/** after. That’s fine if the frontend build output is always there, but fails if build:app didn’t run or outputs elsewhere.

Recommendation: verify the produced dist/ layout matches what your server expects at runtime, and consider adding a clean step (see next point).

5) No clean script; builds can become non-deterministic
You build into dist/ and copy assets into dist/, but there’s no removal of previous build artifacts.

Recommendation: add something like:

clean: remove dist/ before build (using rimraf or del-cli).

6) TypeScript tooling is older and may conflict with newer deps
You’re on "typescript": "^4.8.4" and "ts-node": "^9.1.1". That can work, but if you upgrade Payload ecosystem packages or other dependencies, you may be forced to move TS tooling forward.

Recommendation: if you plan to upgrade Payload packages, expect to upgrade TS and ts-node accordingly.

7) Yarn resolutions pin: check why it’s needed
"resolutions": { "jackspeak": "2.1.1" }
This is often used to patch a transitive vulnerability, but it can also cause unexpected incompatibilities if upstream packages assume a different version.

Recommendation: confirm it’s still necessary:

If it was added for a CVE, verify whether upgrading the parent dependency removes the need.

If you keep it, document it (short note in README) so the next person understands why.

Concrete improvements (minimal changes)
If you want a pragmatic hardening pass without restructuring the repo:

Add clean script and run it at start of build.

Make dev command explicit about what nodemon runs (unless you already have a nodemon.json that does this).

Align and upgrade Payload-related package versions as a set.

Upgrade dotenv.

Validate copyfiles output structure and platform compatibility.

If you paste two more things, I can be precise
If you share:

your nodemon.json (if any) and

your tsconfig.json (and optionally the server entry file path, e.g. src/server.ts)

…I can tell you exactly whether dev and build:server/serve will work as intended, and suggest the exact script changes.

ChatGPT said:
Your config is close, but there are a few real problems that will either break at runtime or bite you during builds.

What to fix
1) serverURL should not be undefined
Right now:

serverURL: process.env.PAYLOAD_DOMAIN,
If PAYLOAD_DOMAIN is not set, Payload ends up with an invalid server URL. Also, Payload typically expects a full URL (including https://), not just a domain.

Fix: provide a safe default and normalize.

Use PAYLOAD_PUBLIC_SERVER_URL (common convention) or keep yours, but ensure it includes protocol.

2) Env vars should be validated
You use:

process.env.DATABASE_URI

process.env.PAYLOAD_DOMAIN

If either is missing, you’ll get confusing errors. Fail fast with clear messages.

3) Webpack fallback block needs a guard
Depending on bundler / webpack version, config.resolve or config.resolve.fallback can be missing. Your spread is fine, but accessing config.resolve.fallback assumes resolve exists.

Fix: ensure config.resolve exists first.

4) Style: inconsistent quotes + semicolon
Not a functional issue, but keep consistency to avoid lint/prettier churn.

A hardened version (drop-in replacement)
import path from 'path'

import { payloadCloud } from '@payloadcms/plugin-cloud'
import { mongooseAdapter } from '@payloadcms/db-mongodb'
import { webpackBundler } from '@payloadcms/bundler-webpack'
import { slateEditor } from '@payloadcms/richtext-slate'
import { buildConfig } from 'payload/config'

import Users from './collections/Users'
import Media from './collections/Media'
import Orders from './collections/Orders'
import Products from './collections/Products'
import Projects from './collections/Projects'

import Pricing from './globals/Pricing'

const SERVER_URL =
  process.env.PAYLOAD_PUBLIC_SERVER_URL ||
  process.env.PAYLOAD_DOMAIN ||
  'http://localhost:3000'

const DATABASE_URI = process.env.DATABASE_URI

if (!DATABASE_URI) {
  throw new Error('Missing env var: DATABASE_URI')
}

// Optional: ensure protocol exists (Payload wants a full URL)
const normalizedServerURL = SERVER_URL.startsWith('http://') || SERVER_URL.startsWith('https://')
  ? SERVER_URL
  : `https://${SERVER_URL}`

export default buildConfig({
  serverURL: normalizedServerURL,
  admin: {
    user: Users.slug,
    bundler: webpackBundler(),
    webpack: (config) => {
      config.resolve = config.resolve || {}
      config.resolve.fallback = {
        ...(config.resolve.fallback || {}),
        zlib: false,
        fs: false,
        'original-fs': false,
      }
      return config
    },
  },
  editor: slateEditor({}),
  collections: [Users, Media, Orders, Products, Projects],
  globals: [Pricing],
  typescript: {
    outputFile: path.resolve(__dirname, 'payload-types.ts'),
  },
  graphQL: {
    schemaOutputFile: path.resolve(__dirname, 'generated-schema.graphql'),
  },
  plugins: [payloadCloud()],
  db: mongooseAdapter({
    url: DATABASE_URI,
  }),
})
Quick checks you should do
Ensure you actually set one of:

PAYLOAD_PUBLIC_SERVER_URL (preferred) or PAYLOAD_DOMAIN
and that it’s correct for local vs production.

Ensure DATABASE_URI is set in the environment where you run dev and serve.

If you paste your src/server.ts (or whatever starts Express/Payload), I can make sure serverURL and your runtime port align correctly.

ChatGPT said:
This file is not valid as-is for Sass in a modern Quasar setup. It mixes Stylus-era imports with Sass syntax, which will fail or behave inconsistently depending on the bundler.

Below is a precise breakdown and a fixed version.

Problems in your file
1) ❌ ~quasar-styl is Stylus, not Sass
@import '~quasar-styl'
quasar-styl was for Stylus (.styl)

You are using Sass indented syntax (.sass)

This import will fail in Vite / Webpack Sass loaders

Same issue with:

// @import '~quasar-addon-styl'
2) ❌ @import is deprecated in Sass
While it still works, Sass now recommends @use / @forward.
Quasar 2 expects Sass variables via @use.

3) ⚠️ Breakpoint variables must come from Quasar Sass
These variables:

$breakpoint-xs
$breakpoint-sm
$breakpoint-md
Only exist if Quasar’s Sass variables are loaded correctly.

Correct Quasar + Sass setup (recommended)
✅ Fixed version (Quasar 2, Sass)
@use 'quasar/src/css/variables.sass' as *
@use 'quasar/src/css/index.sass' as *

@import './quasar.variables.sass'

.scroll-area
  max-height: calc(100vh - 200px)
  overflow-y: auto

  @media (min-width: $breakpoint-sm)
    height: calc(100vh - 200px)

.sm-full-width
  @media (max-width: $breakpoint-sm)
    width: 100%

.grid-4
  display: grid
  grid-template-columns: 1fr
  gap: 16px

  @media (min-width: $breakpoint-xs)
    grid-template-columns: repeat(2, 1fr)

  @media (min-width: $breakpoint-sm)
    grid-template-columns: repeat(3, 1fr)

  @media (min-width: $breakpoint-md)
    grid-template-columns: repeat(4, 1fr)

.q-px-2
  padding-left: 2px
  padding-right: 2px

.q-mx-2
  margin-left: 2px
  margin-right: 2px
Why this works
Uses Quasar’s official Sass entry points

Makes breakpoint variables available

Avoids Stylus-only imports

Compatible with Vite or Webpack

Forward-compatible with modern Sass

One important check
Make sure you have Sass installed, not Stylus:

yarn add -D sass
And remove any Stylus dependencies if present.

Breakpoints	✅ available

ChatGPT said:

Thought for 22s
This error is not coming from your src/styles/quasar.sass syntax. It is triggered when Quasar’s own Sass (QField.sass) is compiled, and your Sass compiler cannot parse Quasar’s selector that uses :has() / :is().

Quasar v2.18.x introduced Sass that requires a newer Sass implementation. The same failure is tracked by Quasar maintainers (the error line matches exactly). 
GitHub
+1

Fix (recommended): upgrade your Sass toolchain
1) Ensure you are using Dart Sass (not node-sass)
If you have node-sass installed anywhere, remove it:

yarn remove node-sass
2) Upgrade Sass + sass-loader (Webpack / Vue CLI)
With Vue CLI + webpack, you should have an explicit Sass compiler in your project. Install/upgrade:

yarn add -D sass@latest sass-loader@latest
3) If your dependency tree includes sass-embedded, upgrade it too
Some Quasar setups pull sass-embedded indirectly; multiple people resolved this exact Quasar :has() selector error by updating sass-embedded. 
GitHub

Do:

yarn add -D sass-embedded@latest
4) Clean reinstall (important)
Old lockfile resolutions commonly keep the broken Sass version around.

rm -rf node_modules yarn.lock
yarn install
Then run your build again.

Workaround (if you cannot upgrade tooling): pin Quasar back
If you cannot move your Sass toolchain forward (for example due to other legacy constraints), pin Quasar to a version before the selector change. The error is reported specifically after upgrading to Quasar 2.18.5+ in webpack projects. 
GitHub

Example:

yarn add quasar@2.17.7
Why your earlier import triggered it
Your src/styles/quasar.sass imports Quasar’s Sass entry (quasar/src/css/index.sass or similar), which pulls in QField.sass. Once it hits the &:has(+ :is(... selector, the older Sass parser throws expected selector. 
GitHub
+1

If you paste your package.json devDependencies related to styling (anything with sass, sass-loader, node-sass, @vue/cli-service, vue-cli-plugin-quasar, webpack) I can tell you exactly which package/version is the culprit and what the minimal upgrade set is for your current stack.

ChatGPT said:
Your Docker build is failing because the Dockerfile has a line that tries to copy yarn.lock, but there is no yarn.lock in the build context (your project directory that you run docker build from):

COPY yarn.lock ./
...
"/yarn.lock": not found
Fix options (pick one)
Option A (best): ensure yarn.lock exists and is committed
From your project root (same folder as the Dockerfile):

yarn install
That will generate yarn.lock (if Yarn is being used). Then rebuild.

Also confirm the file is actually present:

dir yarn.lock
If you are using Git, commit it.

Option B: update your Dockerfile to not require yarn.lock
If you’re not using Yarn, remove the copy line and use npm lockfiles instead.

Replace:

COPY yarn.lock ./
with either nothing (remove the line), or copy npm lockfile:

COPY package-lock.json ./
And use npm in the install step:

RUN npm ci
Option C: make the COPY resilient (supports npm or yarn)
If you want one Dockerfile that works whether yarn.lock exists or not, you can’t do a conditional COPY directly, but you can structure it so you only copy what exists.

A common pattern is:

COPY package.json ./
COPY package-lock.json* yarn.lock* ./
Note the *—this prevents failure if a file is missing.

Then install based on which lockfile is present:

RUN if [ -f yarn.lock ]; then yarn install --frozen-lockfile; else npm ci; fi
This works in Debian/Alpine sh shells.

Also check .dockerignore
Sometimes yarn.lock exists but is excluded from the Docker build context. Open .dockerignore and make sure you do not have a line like:

yarn.lock
If you do, remove it.

What I recommend for your repo
If you use Yarn: generate and commit yarn.lock (Option A).

If you use npm: remove yarn.lock references (Option B).

If you want flexibility: use the wildcard-copy pattern (Option C).

If you paste your Dockerfile, I’ll rewrite it cleanly for your exact build (multi-stage, Payload build, Quasar app build, production runtime).

npm ci is doing exactly what it should: it refuses to run without a lockfile (package-lock.json or npm-shrinkwrap.json). Right now your repo has neither, and earlier Docker failed because it also doesn’t have yarn.lock. In short: you have no lockfile, so neither npm ci nor COPY yarn.lock can succeed.

Correct path forward (choose one)
Option 1 (recommended): Use Yarn (since your Dockerfile expects yarn.lock)
Generate the lockfile:

yarn install
Verify it exists:

dir yarn.lock
Rebuild your docker image.

This is the fastest fix if the project is intended to use Yarn (your scripts also reference yarn).

Option 2: Switch to npm properly (create package-lock.json)
Generate the lockfile:

npm install
Verify:

dir package-lock.json
After that, npm ci will work.

Update your Dockerfile to copy package-lock.json and run npm ci instead of Yarn.

Immediate Dockerfile fix so COPY won’t fail
Even if you haven’t generated lockfiles yet, you should prevent Docker from failing on missing files.

Change:

COPY yarn.lock ./
to:

COPY yarn.lock* ./
COPY package-lock.json* ./
The * makes it non-fatal if the file doesn’t exist.

Then in the install step, use conditional logic:

RUN if [ -f yarn.lock ]; then yarn install --frozen-lockfile; \
    elif [ -f package-lock.json ]; then npm ci; \
    else npm install; fi
This will build even if you’re still transitioning.

Why I recommend Yarn for this repo
Your scripts are written in terms of yarn (e.g., build, build:app, dev:app).

You have a resolutions block, which is a Yarn feature.

So: run yarn install, commit yarn.lock, and your Docker COPY yarn.lock ./ will stop failing.

One more thing to check: .dockerignore
If you run yarn install and the file exists but Docker still can’t copy it, you likely have yarn.lock excluded.

Open .dockerignore and remove any line containing:

yarn.lock

You said:
what is fly secrets set PAYLOAD_SECRET="super-secret-key-change-this"
ChatGPT said:
fly secrets set PAYLOAD_SECRET="super-secret-key-change-this"
is how you define Payload CMS’s application secret in Fly.io’s secure environment store.

It is required by Payload and must be set in production.

What PAYLOAD_SECRET is
In Payload CMS, PAYLOAD_SECRET is a cryptographic secret used to:

Sign and verify JWTs (admin authentication)

Encrypt sensitive values

Secure internal Payload operations

If this variable is:

Missing → Payload will refuse to start in production

Changed later → all existing sessions/tokens become invalid

Think of it like JWT_SECRET or APP_KEY in other frameworks.

Why Fly uses fly secrets set
Fly does not want secrets in:

fly.toml

Dockerfile

source code

.env files committed to Git

Instead, Fly injects them securely at runtime.

When you run:

fly secrets set PAYLOAD_SECRET="super-secret-key-change-this"
Fly:

Encrypts the value

Stores it securely

Exposes it to your app as process.env.PAYLOAD_SECRET

What value should you use?
✅ Use a strong random value
Do NOT use the example string.

On Windows (PowerShell):

node -e "console.log(require('crypto').randomBytes(32).toString('hex'))"
Then:

fly secrets set PAYLOAD_SECRET="PASTE_THE_GENERATED_VALUE"
32 bytes (64 hex chars) is a good minimum.

Required Payload secrets (minimum)
You should set at least these for production:

fly secrets set PAYLOAD_SECRET="..."
fly secrets set DATABASE_URI="mongodb+srv://..."
fly secrets set PAYLOAD_PUBLIC_SERVER_URL="https://ones4app.fly.dev"
Optional but common:

fly secrets set NODE_ENV="production"
How to verify secrets
fly secrets list
You’ll see the names (values are hidden).

Where Payload uses it
Payload reads this automatically. You do not need to reference it manually unless you override defaults.

Internally:

process.env.PAYLOAD_SECRET
Summary
Item	Purpose
PAYLOAD_SECRET	Secures auth + encryption
Where stored	Fly encrypted secrets
How set	fly secrets set
Change later?	❌ breaks sessions
If you want, paste your fly.toml and I’ll verify nothing is accidentally leaking secrets or missing required settings before you deploy.

You said:
downloadable font: Glyph bbox was incorrect (glyph ids 41 75 135) (font-family: "Marck Script" style:normal weight:400 stretch:100 src index:0) source: https://fonts.gstatic.com/s/marckscript/v22/nwpTtK2oNgBA3Or78gapdwuyyCg_.woff2

ChatGPT said:
You are seeing two separate issues:

a Firefox font validation warning for a Google Font (Marck Script)

a WebSocket connection failure to your local dev server (ws://localhost:3040/ws)

They are unrelated, but can appear together during local development.

1) “downloadable font: Glyph bbox was incorrect … Marck Script … woff2”
This is a Firefox-only warning that the font’s internal glyph bounding boxes are slightly off. It is typically caused by how the font was generated and/or how Firefox validates fonts.

What it means:

The font will usually still render.

It is not a network error and not a CORS issue.

It can cause minor text clipping in rare cases, but most of the time it’s harmless.

Practical fixes
Pick the one that matches your needs:

A. Ignore it (most common)
If the font renders fine, you can safely ignore the warning.

B. Self-host the font (recommended for production stability)

Download Marck Script from Google Fonts as a .ttf or .woff2

Serve it from your own app instead of fonts.gstatic.com

This also reduces third-party dependency risk and avoids intermittent font loading issues.

C. Use a different variant/source
Sometimes switching source (e.g., from Google Fonts CSS to a packaged font file) avoids the warning.

2) “Firefox can’t establish a connection to the server at ws://localhost:3040/ws.”
This is the real operational problem. It means your frontend is trying to connect to a WebSocket endpoint on port 3040, and nothing is listening there (or it’s blocked).

Common causes:

Your dev server (Quasar/Vue) is not running on 3040

HMR (hot reload) websocket is configured for 3040 but server runs elsewhere

A proxy is rewriting WS URLs incorrectly

You are running in Docker/Fly where localhost is not the right host

Fix checklist (do these in order)
A) Confirm what is listening on 3040
On Windows PowerShell:

netstat -ano | findstr :3040
If nothing shows up, the websocket target is wrong or the service isn’t running.

B) Confirm your dev server port
If this is Quasar CLI, check quasar.conf.js / quasar.config.js for:

devServer.port

devServer.host

If you expected 3040, force it:

devServer: {
  port: 3040,
  host: 'localhost'
}
If you did not intend 3040, search the repo for it:

findstr /S /N /I "3040" *.*
C) If running inside Docker: localhost is wrong
Inside a container, localhost points to the container itself, not your host machine.

If the browser is on your host and the server is in Docker, you need to expose the port and use the mapped host port.

If the browser is also inside another container, you must use the container service name, not localhost.

D) If this happens on Fly.io: ws://localhost:3040/ws will never work
On Fly, “localhost” is the running VM/container itself, and you typically run one HTTP service behind Fly’s proxy. Any hardcoded dev websocket URL must be disabled in production builds.

What I need from you to pinpoint the exact fix
Paste one of the following and I’ll tell you exactly what to change:

Your Quasar/Vue dev server config (quasar.conf.js / quasar.config.js), especially the devServer block, or

The command/output showing what port your frontend is actually running on, or

The file where /ws is defined (search results for "/ws" or 3040)

If you paste your quasar.config.js devServer section, I can give you the exact correct config for both local dev and Fly production.

ChatGPT said:
If nothing is listening on :3040, then the browser’s ws://localhost:3040/ws attempt is guaranteed to fail. The only question is: who is generating that WebSocket URL (Quasar/Vue dev server HMR, a proxy, or your own code).

Here is the fastest, deterministic way to identify and fix it.

1) Find where 3040 or /ws is coming from
Run both searches from the project root:

PowerShell (Windows)
findstr /S /N /I "3040" *.*
findstr /S /N /I "ws://localhost" *.*
findstr /S /N /I "/ws" *.*
If any of these return a file path, paste the matching lines here.

2) If you are using Quasar/Vue CLI: it’s probably HMR websocket config
Check your Quasar config
Open quasar.conf.js or quasar.config.js and look for:

devServer.port

devServer.client

devServer.webSocketServer

devServer.public

devServer.hmr

devServer.proxy

If port: 3040 is set anywhere, either:

start the dev server on 3040, or

change the config to the actual port you’re using.

If you want the dev server to run on 3040, set it explicitly:

devServer: {
  port: 3040,
  host: 'localhost'
}
Then restart the dev server.

3) If you are NOT running a dev server (production build)
Seeing an HMR websocket URL in a production build means dev-only code/config is leaking into production.

Fix: ensure production build is used
Quasar: use quasar build, not quasar dev

Vue CLI: use yarn build, not yarn serve

And ensure you deploy the compiled output, not the dev server.

4) If you are running behind a proxy (Payload/Express serving the app)
If your backend serves the frontend and you configured a proxy websocket path, remove or correct it.

Typical place:

vue.config.js (Vue CLI)

Quasar devServer.proxy

custom Express proxy middleware

Answer with just A, B, or C (no extra typing required):

ChatGPT said:
Good question. This line must live in the file that actually starts your Node server (the entrypoint Fly runs).

Based on your earlier package.json, that file is:

src/server.ts (compiled to dist/server.js)

Why this file
main → dist/server.js

serve → node dist/server.js

Fly runs the container → starts your server from that file

So the code belongs there, not in Payload config, not in Quasar, not in a route file.

Exactly what your src/server.ts should contain (minimum)
Here is a correct, Fly-compatible skeleton. Compare this to yours and adjust only what’s different.

import express from 'express'
import payload from 'payload'
import dotenv from 'dotenv'

dotenv.config()

const app = express()

const port = process.env.PORT || 8080

app.get('/health', (_req, res) => {
  res.status(200).send('ok')
})

async function start() {
  await payload.init({
    express: app,
    secret: process.env.PAYLOAD_SECRET,
    onInit: () => {
      console.log('Payload initialized')
    },
  })

  app.listen(port, '0.0.0.0', () => {
    console.log(`Server running on port ${port}`)
  })
}

start().catch(err => {
  console.error(err)
  process.exit(1)
})
Key points (important)
Uses process.env.PORT → required by Fly

Binds to 0.0.0.0 → required inside containers

Payload is initialized before listen

dotenv is fine locally; Fly injects env vars automatically

Common mistakes to avoid
❌ Putting app.listen() inside payload.config.ts
❌ Binding to localhost
❌ Hardcoding 3000 only
❌ Having multiple listen() calls

Verify quickly
Open your project and answer these two checks:

Do you have a file at src/server.ts?

Does it contain app.listen(?

If you want zero guesswork, paste your actual src/server.ts here and I will edit it to the exact final version you should deploy.

You said:
are these folders synced?
ChatGPT said:
Short answer: no — they are not automatically synced.

Here’s the clear mental model to keep you from getting stuck.

What is synced vs not synced
✅ Your local folders
C:\Users\Noe\customization\print-designer
These are:

your working copy

what you edit in VS Code

what Git tracks (if you commit)

what Fly reads only when you deploy

They are not live-synced to Fly.

❌ Fly server (remote VM / container)
Fly runs your app inside a container built from your code at deploy time.

Fly does not watch your local files

Fly does not auto-update when you save a file

Fly only updates when you run:

fly deploy
That command:

Sends your current local files to Fly

Builds a Docker image

Replaces the running app with the new version

What this means in practice
If you edit src/server.ts locally:
✔ You must run:

fly deploy
Before the change exists on Fly.

If you edit files on Fly (SSH / console):
❌ Those changes are temporary
❌ They will be lost on the next deploy
❌ This is not how Fly is meant to be used

Fly is immutable infrastructure: deploy replaces everything.

How to think about it (important)
Action	Result
Save file locally	❌ Fly does not see it
Commit to Git	❌ Fly still does not see it
fly deploy	✅ Fly now runs that code
Restart app	❌ Does not pull new code
fly logs	🔍 View runtime output
Correct workflow (always)
Edit files locally

Save

(Optional) yarn build to catch errors

fly deploy

Check:

fly logs
fly open
When things “don’t change”
If you ever think:

“I updated the file but Fly is still behaving the same”

It is almost always because:

you forgot fly deploy, or

the deploy failed and never replaced the app

Check with:

fly status
Next step for you
Now that you understand syncing:

Open src/server.ts

Update app.listen(...) as instructed

Save the file

Run:

fly deploy
If anything fails, paste fly logs output

You’re doing the right steps — this is just the final “cloud mental model” click.

You said:

ChatGPT said:
That is almost correct, but the default port is wrong for Fly.

You do not want a high, hard-coded dev port like 3050 as the fallback in production.

What you have now (problematic)
const port = Number(process.env.PORT) || 3050;
app.listen(port, "0.0.0.0", () => {
Why this can break on Fly
Fly always sets process.env.PORT

If for any reason it is missing or not parsed correctly, your app will bind to 3050

Fly’s proxy will still send traffic to the internal port defined in fly.toml (usually 8080)

Result: app boots, but receives no traffic

Correct version (safe for Fly + local dev)
✅ Recommended
const port = process.env.PORT ? Number(process.env.PORT) : 8080;

app.listen(port, "0.0.0.0", () => {
  console.log(`Server running on port ${port}`);
});
Or (also acceptable)
const port = Number(process.env.PORT || 8080);

app.listen(port, "0.0.0.0", () => {
  console.log(`Server running on port ${port}`);
});
Also confirm fly.toml
Open fly.toml and ensure you have:

[http_service]
  internal_port = 8080
If internal_port is not 3050, then your current fallback is wrong.

Save src/server.ts

You were very close — the binding address was correct (0.0.0.0).

ChatGPT said:
Below is a practical “next steps” checklist to connect Shopify to your app and persist data in MongoDB (Atlas), assuming you’re deploying on Fly and running Payload/Express.

1) Shopify setup
A. Create a Shopify app
In Shopify Admin: Settings → Apps and sales channels → Develop apps.

Create an app (name it for your project).

Configure:

App URL: https://ones4app.fly.dev

Allowed redirection URL(s) (OAuth callback):
https://ones4app.fly.dev/api/shopify/callback
(you can choose a different route; just be consistent in code)

Copy these credentials:

API key (Client ID)

API secret (Client secret)

B. Decide your access model
Single store (private integration): simplest; you install it once for one shop.

Multi-merchant (public app): requires full OAuth flow and per-shop token storage.

Most production integrations should support multi-merchant, even if you start with one store.

C. Choose required scopes
Pick only what you need. Typical:

Orders: read_orders

Products: read_products

Customers: read_customers

Fulfillment/inventory (if needed): read_inventory

Webhooks (recommended): you’ll register webhook subscriptions via API.

D. Set up webhooks (recommended)
At minimum:

orders/create, orders/updated, orders/cancelled

products/create, products/update, products/delete

Webhooks keep Mongo in sync without polling.

2) MongoDB setup
A. Create MongoDB Atlas (if not already)
Create cluster, database user, and network access.

Network Access:

If you want quick success: allow 0.0.0.0/0 temporarily (not ideal).

Preferably: lock down later; Fly egress IPs are not stable unless you use dedicated egress.

Get your connection string:

mongodb+srv://USER:PASSWORD@clustername.mongodb.net/dbname?...

B. Store Mongo URI in Fly secrets
fly secrets set DATABASE_URI="mongodb+srv://..."
C. Define what you store
Minimum recommended collections for Shopify integration:

shops (one per Shopify store)

shopDomain (e.g., my-shop.myshopify.com)

accessToken (encrypted at rest, never logged)

scopes, installedAt, uninstalledAt

orders

shopDomain, shopifyOrderId, orderNumber, financialStatus, totals, line items, timestamps

products

shopDomain, shopifyProductId, title, variants, images, timestamps

webhook_events (optional but useful)

topic, shopDomain, webhookId, payload hash, receivedAt, processedAt, status, retryCount

Add indexes:

shops.shopDomain unique

orders.shopDomain + orders.shopifyOrderId unique

products.shopDomain + products.shopifyProductId unique

3) Application work (what to implement)
A. OAuth install flow (multi-merchant)
Endpoints:

GET /api/shopify/install?shop=...
Redirects to Shopify authorization screen.

GET /api/shopify/callback
Exchanges code for access token; stores token in Mongo under shops.

Critical security:

Verify hmac on callback.

Use state nonce to prevent CSRF.

Never store secrets in the frontend.

B. Webhook receiver endpoint
POST /api/shopify/webhooks

Verify webhook HMAC signature

Persist event record

Process event (sync order/product) and mark processed

C. Initial backfill job
After install, you typically do a one-time sync:

Fetch existing products/orders via Shopify Admin API

Store in Mongo

Then rely on webhooks for ongoing updates

4) Production environment variables (Fly)
In addition to DATABASE_URI, set:

fly secrets set SHOPIFY_API_KEY="..."
fly secrets set SHOPIFY_API_SECRET="..."
fly secrets set SHOPIFY_SCOPES="read_orders,read_products,read_customers"
fly secrets set SHOPIFY_APP_URL="https://ones4app.fly.dev"
fly secrets set PAYLOAD_SECRET="..."
fly secrets set PAYLOAD_PUBLIC_SERVER_URL="https://ones4app.fly.dev"
If you process webhooks asynchronously, also consider:

WEBHOOK_SIGNING_SECRET (if you separate concerns)

Queue configuration (optional)

5) Validation checklist
Install app on a dev store:

/api/shopify/install?shop=your-shop.myshopify.com

Confirm a shops record exists with token.

Create an order/product:

Confirm webhook hits your endpoint (fly logs)

Confirm Mongo records updated

Restart app:

Confirm it still reads token from Mongo and continues processing webhooks.

ChatGPT said:
Great question — do not click “Create database” manually yet unless you want to.
You already have the correct database (print-designer). Now you need to decide who creates the collections: Payload / your server (recommended) or you manually.

Below is the clean, production-safe approach.

✅ Correct approach (recommended)
Let your app create the collections automatically

MongoDB is schema-less. Collections and indexes are created when your code first writes data.

Because you are using Payload + Mongoose, this is what should happen:

Database: print-designer ✅ (already exists)

Collections: created automatically on first insert

Indexes: created via schema definitions

👉 You do NOT need to click “Create database”
👉 You do NOT need to manually create collections

Step 1 — Confirm your app is using the right DB
Your DATABASE_URI should end with:

/print-designer
Example:

mongodb+srv://user:pass@cluster0.xxxx.mongodb.net/print-designer
Verify:

fly secrets list
If it points to test or admin, fix it now.

Step 2 — Define collections in code (THIS is the key step)
You need a Shopify data layer (Payload collection or Mongoose model).

You have two valid options:

OPTION A (Best): Use Payload collections
Since you already use Payload, define Shopify collections there.

src/collections/Shops.ts
import { CollectionConfig } from 'payload/types'

const Shops: CollectionConfig = {
  slug: 'shops',
  admin: { useAsTitle: 'shopDomain' },
  fields: [
    { name: 'shopDomain', type: 'text', required: true, unique: true },
    { name: 'accessToken', type: 'text', required: true },
    { name: 'scopes', type: 'text' },
    { name: 'installedAt', type: 'date' },
    { name: 'uninstalledAt', type: 'date' },
  ],
}

export default Shops
Add to payload.config.ts:

collections: [
  Users,
  Media,
  Orders,
  Products,
  Projects,
  Shops, // 👈
]
Payload will:

Create the shops collection

Enforce uniqueness on shopDomain

Show data in admin UI

Orders & Products (simplified)
You can extend later — start minimal.

{
  name: 'shopDomain',
  type: 'text',
  index: true,
},
{
  name: 'shopifyId',
  type: 'number',
  index: true,
},
Payload automatically creates indexes.

OPTION B: Use raw Mongo / Mongoose models
Only do this if you do not want Shopify data in Payload Admin.

Example:

db.collection('shops').createIndex(
  { shopDomain: 1 },
  { unique: true }
)
But since you already use Payload → Option A is strongly recommended.

Step 3 — Do NOT manually create indexes in Atlas
Because:

Payload/Mongoose will manage them

Manual indexes can conflict during deploys

Fly restarts can cause drift

Step 4 — Test creation (this confirms everything)
Once you:

Add the shops collection

Deploy

Run Shopify install

Payload will automatically insert a document like:

{
  "shopDomain": "your-shop.myshopify.com",
  "accessToken": "shpat_...",
  "installedAt": "2025-12-31T..."
}
👉 Then Atlas will immediately show:

shops

products (later)

Short answer first:

Define the collections in your app (code) → deploy to Fly.

Now the precise breakdown so there’s no ambiguity.

Where each thing lives (clear separation)
MongoDB Atlas (DB)
Purpose: storage only

You should NOT:

Click “Create database”

Manually create collections

Manually create indexes

Atlas will:

Automatically create databases & collections when your app writes data

Enforce indexes defined by your schemas

Your app code (local → Fly)
Purpose: structure + logic

You DO:

Define collections/models in code

Define indexes & uniqueness in code

Control what gets written

This code runs on Fly.

So when you asked “on DB or Fly?” — here is the exact answer
Task	Where
Define shops, orders, products	App code (Payload collections)
Indexes (unique, compound)	App code
Create database	Automatic (Mongo)
Create collections	Automatic (Mongo on first write)
Run the app	Fly

You said:
Cannot GET /admin
ChatGPT said:

Thought for 11s
Cannot GET /admin means your Fly app is responding, but the Payload Admin route is not mounted (i.e., Payload didn’t initialize on that running process, or it’s not serving the admin bundle in production).

Here is the fastest way to isolate and fix it.

1) Check whether Payload is actually initializing on Fly
Run:

fly logs
You are looking for one of these patterns:

Good signs:

Payload initialized

Admin URL: or similar startup output

Bad signs (most common):

Mongo connection/auth errors

Missing env errors (PAYLOAD_SECRET, DATABASE_URI, etc.)

“Cannot find module dist/payload.config.js” / wrong config path

If Payload never initializes, /admin will 404.

2) Confirm your server entry actually calls payload.init(...)
Open src/server.ts (and confirm the compiled version is in dist/server.js after build).

A correct production server must do something like this:

import express from 'express'
import payload from 'payload'

const app = express()

async function start() {
  await payload.init({
    express: app,
    secret: process.env.PAYLOAD_SECRET,
  })

  const port = Number(process.env.PORT || 8080)
  app.listen(port, '0.0.0.0')
}

start()
If you have app.listen(...) but no payload.init({ express: app, ... }), you will get Cannot GET /admin.

3) Verify Fly is using your production “serve” command
Your package.json has:

"serve": "cross-env PAYLOAD_CONFIG_PATH=dist/payload.config.js NODE_ENV=production node dist/server.js"
That is correct only if:

dist/payload.config.js exists in the final image

dist/server.js exists

Fly runs yarn serve (or equivalent)

Quick verification on Fly
Run:

fly ssh console -C "ls -la dist | head -50"
You should see:

dist/server.js

dist/payload.config.js

If dist/payload.config.js is missing, Payload will not boot.

4) The most common root cause in your project: payload config not compiled into dist/
This happens when:

tsconfig.json excludes the file

or the file is in the wrong location

or the Docker build copies the wrong paths

Fix
Ensure your real config is at:

src/payload.config.ts
And ensure TypeScript compiles it to:

dist/payload.config.js
Locally, verify:

yarn build:server
dir dist | findstr payload.config
5) After the fix
Deploy again:

fly deploy
fly open
Then try:

https://ones4app.fly.dev/admin

If you paste ONE thing, I can tell you the exact fix immediately
Paste the output of:

fly logs
(Just the part around startup.)
From that, I can tell you whether this is (a) missing env vars, (b) missing dist/payload.config.js, (c) server not calling payload.init, or (d) DB connection failure preventing Payload from mounting routes.

ChatGPT said:
Your logs show the root cause precisely:

Payload is starting correctly.

But your server is listening on localhost:3050:
Server listening on http://localhost:3050

Fly is configured to route to internal_port = 8080 (from your fly.toml).

So Fly is probing 0.0.0.0:8080 and your app is not there → connection refused → you end up with broken routing and “Cannot GET /admin”.

Fix: make Fly and your server use the same port (recommended: 8080)
You have two safe ways. Pick Option A (best practice) unless you have a reason not to.

Option A (recommended): Change the server to listen on process.env.PORT (Fly sets it to 8080) and bind to 0.0.0.0
In your server file (likely src/server.ts), change the listen section to:

const port = Number(process.env.PORT || 8080)
app.listen(port, '0.0.0.0', () => {
  console.log(`Server listening on http://0.0.0.0:${port}`)
})
Important:

Remove any hardcoded 3050.

Keep 0.0.0.0 (not localhost).

Then rebuild and deploy:

fly deploy
After deploy, run:

fly logs
You should now see “Server listening on …:8080” (or at least the port shown should match process.env.PORT).

Option B: Change Fly to use 3050 (not recommended, but works)
Edit fly.toml:

[http_service]
  internal_port = 3050
Then deploy:

fly deploy
I recommend Option A because it matches how Fly expects apps to run and avoids surprises later.

Also important: Fly trial is stopping your machine after 5 minutes
Your logs show:
Trial machine stopping... add a credit card...

If you don’t add a card, the app will keep shutting down and you’ll see intermittent failures even after fixing the port.

What to do now (exact next steps)
Implement Option A in src/server.ts (replace the hardcoded 3050).

fly deploy

https://ones4app.fly.dev/admin

You said:
where else I need to update the port to 8080
ChatGPT said:
You only need one port in production: the one Fly routes to. To use 8080, update these places (in this order). After this, Fly will stop complaining and /admin will load.

1) fly.toml (Fly’s router target)
Set Fly to route traffic to 8080:

[http_service]
  internal_port = 8080
This is the port Fly expects inside the container.

2) Your server app.listen(...) (the actual listener)
In the file that becomes dist/server.js (usually src/server.ts), make it listen on Fly’s port:

const port = Number(process.env.PORT || 8080)
app.listen(port, '0.0.0.0', () => {
  console.log(`Server listening on http://0.0.0.0:${port}`)
})
Key requirements:

must bind to 0.0.0.0

must use process.env.PORT (Fly sets this)

default to 8080

3) Your Docker / Fly start command (so it doesn’t force a different port)
Check your Dockerfile / entry command and make sure you are not hardcoding 3050 anywhere.

Also ensure your production start uses your “serve” script (recommended). In Fly, it’s normally:

CMD ["node", "dist/server.js"] or

yarn serve

If your server code is correct, either is fine—but don’t set PORT=3050 anywhere.

4) Remove any other “PORT=3050” overrides
Search your project for 3050 and PORT=:

PowerShell

findstr /S /I "3050 PORT=" *.*
Common places you might find it:

.env (local only, should not affect Fly unless you copied it into secrets)

src/server.ts

src/app config (frontend dev server only)

Dockerfile / fly launch generated start command

On Fly, the only “PORT” that matters is the environment variable Fly provides.

5) Redeploy and verify from logs
Deploy:

fly deploy
Then:

fly logs --tail
You should see something like:

“Server listening on … :8080”

Fly proxy should stop saying “refused connection”.

Important note about Payload’s log line
Payload logging “Server listening on http://localhost:3050” is coming from your code (or a default). After you change app.listen and redeploy, that log line must change too. If it doesn’t, you edited the wrong file.

If you want the exact file location
Run this in your project root and paste the results:

dir .\src -Recurse server.ts
findstr /S /I "listen(" .\src\*.ts
I’ll tell you exactly which file is your real entrypoint and exactly what to change.

This log tells us exactly what is wrong:

Your app is still listening on localhost:3050 (inside the container).

Fly is routing/probing 0.0.0.0:8080.

Even if the port matched, binding to localhost inside a container makes it unreachable from Fly proxy. You must bind to 0.0.0.0.

So you need to update two things: (1) bind address, (2) port alignment.

Below is the minimal, correct fix to use 8080.

1) Update fly.toml to use 8080
In fly.toml:

[http_service]
  internal_port = 8080

You are extremely close. The server is binding correctly (0.0.0.0), but your log line is misleading and—more importantly—Fly is still probing the wrong port because your running container is still on 3050 (meaning either PORT is set to 3050 in Fly, or the compiled dist/server.js still has 3050 logic, or fly.toml internal_port doesn’t match what you expect).

Here is the exact set of fixes to apply.

A) Fix the log line (cosmetic but removes confusion)
Change this:

payload.logger.info(`Server listening on http://localhost:${port}`);
To this:

payload.logger.info(`Server listening on http://0.0.0.0:${port}`);
This does not change behavior, but it removes the “localhost” confusion in logs.

B) Make the port logic Fly-safe
Right now you have:

const port = Number(process.env.PORT) || 8080;
That is fine, but it will use 3050 if Fly has PORT=3050 set anywhere. Make it explicit and robust:

const port = Number(process.env.PORT || 8080);
This avoids Number(undefined) edge cases and is the conventional pattern.

Full corrected block:

const port = Number(process.env.PORT || 8080);

app.listen(port, "0.0.0.0", () => {
  payload.logger.info(`Server listening on http://0.0.0.0:${port}`);
});
C) Ensure Fly routes to the same port your app listens on
You must choose one of these two approaches.

Option 1 (recommended): Use 8080 everywhere
fly.toml:

[http_service]
  internal_port = 8080
Remove/override any Fly secret that sets PORT to 3050:
Run:

fly secrets list
If you see PORT set, it may be 3050. Fix it:

fly secrets set PORT=8080
(Or if you prefer, remove it: fly secrets unset PORT.)

Redeploy:

fly deploy
Option 2: Keep your server on 3050 (easiest if Fly is already using it)
If you want to keep the server at 3050, then change fly.toml to:

[http_service]
  internal_port = 3050
Redeploy. Done.

D) Confirm what port Fly is actually expecting on the machine
After deploy:

fly logs --tail
and also run:

fly status
If Fly still complains about 0.0.0.0:8080, your fly.toml still says 8080.

If Fly complains about 0.0.0.0:3050, then it expects 3050.

E) One more likely issue: your root route may be breaking Payload admin
You are serving "/" static + app.get("/") which is fine, but it can sometimes confuse routing if your SPA catch-all becomes too aggressive.

To be safe, only serve the SPA if the file exists; otherwise let Payload handle routes:

import fs from "fs";

const appDistPath = path.join(__dirname, "app/dist");
if (fs.existsSync(path.join(appDistPath, "index.html"))) {
  app.use("/", express.static(appDistPath));
  app.get("/", (req, res) => {
    res.sendFile(path.join(appDistPath, "index.html"));
  });
}
This prevents accidental “Cannot GET /admin” scenarios if your build output isn’t present or routing collides.

ChatGPT said:
That Payload Admin URL: http://localhost:3050/admin line is coming from Payload itself (specifically payload.getAdminURL()), and it is built from your serverURL setting in payload.config (and/or defaults). It is not “where the admin lives” so much as “what URL Payload thinks it should advertise.”

Where to find and fix it
In your project, open:

src/payload.config.ts (you already pasted it earlier)

You currently have something like:

export default buildConfig({
  serverURL: process.env.PAYLOAD_DOMAIN,
  ...
})
If PAYLOAD_DOMAIN is not set, Payload will fall back and you’ll see localhost in logs.

Fix: set serverURL to your Fly domain in production
Update serverURL to use an environment variable that includes the full https URL, and keep localhost for local dev:

serverURL: process.env.PAYLOAD_PUBLIC_SERVER_URL || 'http://localhost:3050',
Then, in Fly, set it to your real domain:

fly secrets set PAYLOAD_PUBLIC_SERVER_URL=https://ones4app.fly.dev
Redeploy:

fly deploy
After that, logs should show:

Payload Admin URL: https://ones4app.fly.dev/admin

Also: why it still says port 3050
That means your server is still running with PORT=3050 in that environment. To confirm and fix:

fly secrets list
If PORT exists and is 3050, set it:

fly secrets set PORT=8080
And make sure fly.toml matches:

[http_service]
  internal_port = 8080
What “payload admin” actually is
The admin UI is always at:

https://<your-serverURL>/admin

So once serverURL is correct, that printed Admin URL will be correct too.

If you paste your current src/payload.config.ts serverURL line and your fly secrets list output (just keys, no values), I’ll tell you exactly what to change and in what order.

ChatGPT said:
This error is pure TypeScript syntax/order, not Fly and not Mongo.

What’s happening
payload build (via SWC) is compiling src/payload.config.ts and it found this:

collections: [
  Users,
  Media,
  Orders,
  Products,
  Projects,
  Shops,
],
import Pricing from './globals/Pricing'
That import Pricing... is below executable code / object literal, which is invalid in TS/JS modules. Imports must be at the top-level, before other statements.

Fix (do this exactly)
1) Move import Pricing... to the top with your other imports
Open: src/payload.config.ts

At the top, you should have something like:

import path from 'path'
import { payloadCloud } from '@payloadcms/plugin-cloud'
import { mongooseAdapter } from '@payloadcms/db-mongodb'
import { webpackBundler } from '@payloadcms/bundler-webpack'
import { slateEditor } from '@payloadcms/richtext-slate'
import { buildConfig } from 'payload/config'

import Users from './collections/Users'
import Media from './collections/Media'
import Orders from './collections/Orders'
import Products from './collections/Products'
import Projects from './collections/Projects'
import Shops from './collections/Shops'

import Pricing from './globals/Pricing'   // <-- MUST be here
2) Ensure globals and collections stay inside buildConfig
Example:

export default buildConfig({
  // ...
  collections: [Users, Media, Orders, Products, Projects, Shops],
  globals: [Pricing],
  // ...
})
3) Rebuild locally first
Run:

yarn build:payload
If that passes, then deploy:

fly deploy
Why SWC says “Expected . or (”
Because once SWC is parsing the file and encounters import Pricing... after it has already started parsing expressions, it treats import as if it were an identifier/expression in the wrong place, and the next token (Pricing) triggers the “unexpected token” message.

Quick checklist if it still fails
Make sure there are no imports anywhere except the very top of the file.

Make sure you didn’t accidentally paste import Pricing... inside the config object or inside a collections: [...] array.

Confirm Shops import exists and the file path is correct.

If you paste your current src/payload.config.ts (first ~60 lines), I can tell you the exact line that’s out of place.

This means inside the Docker/Fly build, the file path you’re importing does not exist exactly as written. On Windows you can “get away with” case mismatches; Linux (Fly) is case-sensitive, so it fails.

1) Confirm the real filename and folder name
In your project root (same level as src), run:

PowerShell

You are looking for the exact file name, for example:

shops/index.ts

import Shops from './collections/shops'
If the file is src/collections/Shops.ts
import Shops from './collections/Shops'
If it’s a folder src/collections/Shops/index.ts
import Shops from './collections/Shops'
If it’s a folder src/collections/shops/index.ts
import Shops from './collections/shops'
3) Confirm the file exports a default

yarn build:payload

But Payload, TypeScript, and most codebases use PascalCase for collections, and mixing styles increases future errors.

Why this matters
Windows: case-insensitive → hides bugs

Linux (Fly): case-sensitive → fails hard

CI/CD and Docker always behave like Linux

ChatGPT said:
Those are warnings, not the cause of your deploy failure. Your earlier failures were hard errors (port mismatch and missing module / config syntax). Here’s how to interpret each warning and when to act.

1) html-webpack-plugin … unmet peer dependency "webpack@^5.20.0"
2) terser-webpack-plugin … unmet peer dependency "webpack@^5.1.0"
Meaning: Some dependency expects webpack 5 to exist at the top level.
Impact: Usually none if your build completes. Payload (and your app) likely bring webpack via another path or your bundler handles it.

Action: Ignore unless you get runtime/build errors about webpack being missing.
If you do get webpack-related build errors, add webpack explicitly:

yarn add -D webpack@^5 webpack-cli@^5
(Do not do this unless you actually see webpack failures.)

3) @types/react-transition-group … unmet peer dependency "@types/react@*"
Meaning: A types package is expecting React type defs in the project.
Impact: If you’re not compiling TS that needs those types, it’s harmless.

Action: Ignore unless TypeScript errors mention missing React types.
If you do see TS errors, fix with:

yarn add -D @types/react @types/react-dom
4) Workspaces can only be enabled in private projects
Meaning: Your package.json has workspaces, but the project is not marked "private": true.

Impact: This can affect installs/hoisting and occasionally breaks monorepo installs in CI/Docker.

Action (recommended): In the root package.json, add:

{
  "private": true
}
This does not publish anything; it just tells Yarn “treat this as a workspace root.”

What you should focus on (based on your logs)
A) Fly port mismatch
Your app must listen on the same port Fly expects.

You currently have:

fly.toml internal_port = 8080

logs show server listening on 3050

So you must ensure your server uses:

const port = Number(process.env.PORT) || 8080
app.listen(port, "0.0.0.0", ...)
…and that PORT is set / passed correctly at runtime.

B) Module resolution + casing
Your “Cannot find module './collections/Shops'” is a Linux case-sensitivity issue unless the file truly doesn’t exist.

Minimal “safe” checklist before the next deploy
Root package.json: add "private": true

Ensure src/collections/Shops.ts exists with exact casing

Ensure collections: [..., Shops] uses the imported Shops identifier

Ensure your server listens on process.env.PORT (or default 8080)

ChatGPT said:
Below is a clear, end-to-end explanation of Step 3–5 (Shopify OAuth install → webhooks → verify MongoDB), written so you can execute it exactly.

Step 3 — Shopify OAuth install flow (what it is and how you test it)
What this step is doing
This is the “install” process for a Shopify app:

Your app sends the merchant (your dev store) to Shopify’s permission screen.

Merchant clicks Install (approves scopes).

Shopify redirects back to your app’s callback URL with a temporary code.

Your app exchanges that code for a permanent access token.

Your app saves that token + shop domain into MongoDB (your shops collection).

What you do now (the test install URL)
You open this in a browser:

https://ones4app.fly.dev/api/shopify/install?shop=your-store.myshopify.com
Replace your-store.myshopify.com with your real dev store domain, for example:

https://ones4app.fly.dev/api/shopify/install?shop=ones4-dev.myshopify.com
Expected behavior (what you should see)
Redirect to Shopify permission screen
You’ll see Shopify asking to approve permissions for your app.

You click Install (or Approve).

Shopify redirects to your callback endpoint:

https://ones4app.fly.dev/api/shopify/callback?code=...&shop=...&state=...
Your backend should log something like:

“OAuth callback received”

“Exchanged code for token”

“Saved shop to MongoDB”

If you do NOT get the expected redirects
Common reasons (high probability):

Your Shopify app settings don’t match your deployed domain

Wrong callback URL in Shopify Partners / App settings

Missing or invalid shop parameter

Your server isn’t reachable on the correct Fly port (but you said Fly is accessible now)

Shopify settings you must have correct
In your Shopify app config (Partners dashboard / App setup):

App URL: https://ones4app.fly.dev

Allowed redirection URL(s) must include your callback endpoint, e.g.
https://ones4app.fly.dev/api/shopify/callback

Step 4 — Webhooks (recommended) to keep MongoDB synced
What webhooks do
Webhooks are Shopify’s “push notifications.” Instead of you polling Shopify every few minutes, Shopify automatically calls your endpoint when something changes.

You listed:

orders/create

orders/updated

products/update

This means:

When an order is created/updated, Shopify sends a POST to your app

When a product changes, Shopify sends a POST to your app

Your app then updates MongoDB accordingly (orders/products collections)

When to register webhooks
Register webhooks immediately after a successful install, because you now have:

shop domain

access token (needed to call Shopify Admin API to register webhooks)

Typical flow:

Install success (callback finishes)

Save shop + token to MongoDB

Call Shopify Admin API to register webhooks for that shop

Save webhook IDs (optional but recommended)

What you need configured
In Shopify, each webhook must point to a public HTTPS endpoint, e.g.:

https://ones4app.fly.dev/api/webhooks/orders-create

https://ones4app.fly.dev/api/webhooks/orders-updated

https://ones4app.fly.dev/api/webhooks/products-update

Also, your webhook endpoints must:

Read the raw request body (Shopify signature verification requires raw body)

Verify X-Shopify-Hmac-Sha256 signature

Respond quickly (Shopify expects a fast 200 OK)

Then process and write to MongoDB (or queue and process)

Expected outcome
After you register them, Shopify will start sending events automatically and your MongoDB stays in sync without manual pulls.

Step 5 — Verify MongoDB (Atlas) saved shop document
What you are checking
After the install flow succeeds, your app should save a document like this:

Database: print-designer
Collection: shops

Example fields you should see:

shopDomain: your-store.myshopify.com

accessToken: (encrypted or at least not logged)

scopes: (string list)

installedAt: timestamp

How to verify in MongoDB Atlas
Open MongoDB Atlas

Go to your cluster → Browse Collections

Select:

Database: print-designer

Collection: shops

Confirm there is one document for your dev store

If nothing shows up
That means the callback did not complete successfully, or the database write failed. In that case:

check Fly logs for the callback endpoint

confirm DATABASE_URI is set in Fly secrets

confirm your callback is actually being hit

Quick “success checklist” (what “done” looks like)
Opening /api/shopify/install?shop=... sends you to Shopify permission screen

After approval, you land back on /api/shopify/callback

Fly logs show “saved shop”

MongoDB Atlas print-designer.shops has the shop document

Webhooks registered (you either see IDs stored, or you confirm in Shopify admin webhook deliveries)

If you paste (1) your dev store domain and (2) the route names you currently have (exact paths for /api/shopify/install and /api/shopify/callback and your webhook endpoints), I can tell you the exact URLs to enter in Shopify and exactly what you should see in logs at each point.

Debugging permissions, scopes, and API calls

A dev store:

Is free

Has no billing

Allows installing unpublished / custom apps

Can be reset or deleted without risk

👉 This is the correct and recommended setup for your current stage.

❌ Real / production Shopify store
Do not use a live store yet because:

OAuth mistakes can lock merchants out

Installed on real merchant stores

Billing enabled (if applicable)

One important Shopify detail (very important)
A Shopify app is tied to a single store at install time.

That means:

You install the same app on many stores

Each store gets its own access token

Each store gets its own document in MongoDB (shops collection)

So starting with a dev store is not “throwaway work” — it’s the exact same flow you’ll use later.

Step 2 — Implement the real /install redirect (OAuth start)

generates a state value

builds the Shopify authorization URL

redirects the browser to Shopify

In src/routes/shopify.ts, your /install should end with a redirect to:

https://{shop}/admin/oauth/authorize?...

Parameters:

client_id = your API key

scope = your scopes

redirect_uri = https://ones4app.fly.dev/api/shopify/callback

state = random string

grant_options[]=per-user (optional; usually omit unless you’re doing per-user tokens)

Also: store state temporarily (cookie or your DB). Cookie is simplest.

Step 3 — Implement /callback (OAuth finish)
Callback must:

read shop, code, state, hmac from query

verify state matches what you stored

verify hmac using your SHOPIFY_API_SECRET

exchange code for access_token by POSTing to:
https://{shop}/admin/oauth/access_token

save { shopDomain, accessToken, scopes, installedAt } to MongoDB (your shops collection)

Then redirect to something like:

/admin (Payload Admin) or

a success page like /installed

ChatGPT said:
Good — your /api/shopify/install route is reachable. Right now it’s only returning a test message; it is not yet starting OAuth, so you will not see the Shopify permission screen until we add the redirect logic.

Below is the exact “next” implementation you need: /install → redirect to Shopify, then /callback → exchange code for token + save shop in Mongo.

1) Install route: redirect to Shopify OAuth screen
Create (or edit) this file:

src/routes/shopify.ts (or wherever you keep Express routes)

import { Router } from "express";
import crypto from "crypto";

const router = Router();

router.get("/install", (req, res) => {
  const shop = String(req.query.shop || "").trim();

  if (!shop || !shop.endsWith(".myshopify.com")) {
    return res.status(400).send("Missing or invalid ?shop=");
  }

  const apiKey = process.env.SHOPIFY_API_KEY!;
  const appUrl = process.env.SHOPIFY_APP_URL!; // https://ones4app.fly.dev
  const scopes = process.env.SHOPIFY_SCOPES!;  // read_products,read_orders

  if (!apiKey || !appUrl || !scopes) {
    return res.status(500).send("Missing Shopify env vars");
  }

  const state = crypto.randomBytes(16).toString("hex");

  // Store state temporarily (cookie is simplest for now)
  res.cookie("shopify_state", state, {
    httpOnly: true,
    secure: true,
    sameSite: "lax",
  });

  const redirectUri = `${appUrl}/api/shopify/callback`;

  const installUrl =
    `https://${shop}/admin/oauth/authorize` +
    `?client_id=${encodeURIComponent(apiKey)}` +
    `&scope=${encodeURIComponent(scopes)}` +
    `&redirect_uri=${encodeURIComponent(redirectUri)}` +
    `&state=${encodeURIComponent(state)}`;

  return res.redirect(installUrl);
});

export default router;
Important:

This uses cookies, so you must have cookie parsing + Express “trust proxy” set correctly (next step).

2) Callback route: exchange code → access token
Add to the same router:

router.get("/callback", async (req, res) => {
  const { shop, code, state } = req.query as Record<string, string>;

  if (!shop || !code || !state) return res.status(400).send("Missing params");

  const expectedState = req.cookies?.shopify_state;
  if (!expectedState || expectedState !== state) {
    return res.status(400).send("Invalid state");
  }

  const apiSecret = process.env.SHOPIFY_API_SECRET!;
  const apiKey = process.env.SHOPIFY_API_KEY!;

  const tokenRes = await fetch(`https://${shop}/admin/oauth/access_token`, {
    method: "POST",
    headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json" },
    body: JSON.stringify({
      client_id: apiKey,
      client_secret: apiSecret,
      code,
    }),
  });

  if (!tokenRes.ok) {
    const txt = await tokenRes.text();
    return res.status(500).send(`Token exchange failed: ${txt}`);
  }

  const tokenJson = (await tokenRes.json()) as { access_token: string };
  const accessToken = tokenJson.access_token;

  // Save to Mongo via Payload collection "Shops" (recommended)
  // If you have a Payload collection named Shops with fields shopDomain/accessToken, do:
  const payload = req.app.get("payload");
  if (!payload) return res.status(500).send("Payload not found on app");

  await payload.create({
    collection: "shops",

      shopDomain: shop,
      accessToken,
      installedAt: new Date().toISOString(),
      scopes: process.env.SHOPIFY_SCOPES,
    },
  });

  return res.send(`Installed OK for ${shop}`);
});

You said:
Invalid state
ChatGPT said:
“Invalid state” means the value you sent to Shopify in state=... during /install does not match what you received back on /callback (or you didn’t persist it correctly). This is a required CSRF protection check.

Here is the reliable way to fix it on Fly (stateless, works across machines):

Fix: store state in a signed cookie (recommended)
1) Install cookie parser
yarn add cookie-parser
2) In your server entry (before routes)
import cookieParser from "cookie-parser";

Add this to Fly secrets:

3) In /install: generate + set cookie + redirect
import crypto from "crypto";

router.get("/install", (req, res) => {
  const shop = String(req.query.shop || "");
  if (!shop) return res.status(400).send("Missing shop");

  const state = crypto.randomBytes(16).toString("hex");

  res.cookie("shopify_oauth_state", state, {
    httpOnly: true,
    secure: true,
    sameSite: "lax",
    signed: true,
    maxAge: 10 * 60 * 1000, // 10 min
  });

  // build installUrl with state=...
  res.redirect(installUrl);
});
4) In /callback: verify cookie state matches query state
router.get("/callback", async (req, res) => {
  const { shop, state, code } = req.query as Record<string, string>;

  const savedState = req.signedCookies?.shopify_oauth_state;
  if (!savedState || !state || savedState !== state) {
    return res.status(400).send("Invalid state");
  }

  // optional: clear cookie after success
  res.clearCookie("shopify_oauth_state");

  // exchange `code` -> access token (next step)
  // save token in Mongo
  res.send("State OK. Ready to exchange code.");
});
Common reasons you still get “Invalid state”
You didn’t mount cookieParser / trust proxy before routes.

You’re testing install in one browser tab, then callback in another session (cookie missing).

You used sameSite: "strict" (can block redirect cookies). Use "lax".

You’re hitting http:// somewhere. Use only https://ones4app.fly.dev/....

You have multiple Fly machines and you used memory storage for state (won’t work). Cookie fix avoids this.

Quick check (fast)

import { Router } from "express";
import crypto from "crypto";

const router = Router();

/**
 * STEP 3 — START OAUTH (INSTALL)
 * URL: /api/shopify/install?shop=xxx.myshopify.com
 */
router.get("/install", (req, res) => {
  const shop = String(req.query.shop || "").trim();

  if (!shop || !shop.endsWith(".myshopify.com")) {
    return res.status(400).send("Invalid shop");
  }

  const apiKey = process.env.SHOPIFY_API_KEY!;
  const appUrl = process.env.SHOPIFY_APP_URL!;
  const scopes = process.env.SHOPIFY_SCOPES!;

  if (!apiKey || !appUrl || !scopes) {
    return res.status(500).send("Missing Shopify env vars");
  }

  // 1. Generate CSRF state
  const state = crypto.randomBytes(16).toString("hex");

  // 2. Save state in signed cookie
  res.cookie("shopify_oauth_state", state, {
    httpOnly: true,
    secure: true,
    sameSite: "lax",
    signed: true,
    maxAge: 10 * 60 * 1000,
  });

  // 3. Redirect to Shopify
  const redirectUri = `${appUrl}/api/shopify/callback`;

  const installUrl =
    `https://${shop}/admin/oauth/authorize` +
    `?client_id=${encodeURIComponent(apiKey)}` +
    `&scope=${encodeURIComponent(scopes)}` +
    `&redirect_uri=${encodeURIComponent(redirectUri)}` +
    `&state=${encodeURIComponent(state)}`;

  return res.redirect(installUrl);
});

/**
 * STEP 4 — FINISH OAUTH (CALLBACK)
 * URL: /api/shopify/callback
 */
router.get("/callback", async (req, res) => {
  const { shop, code, state } = req.query as Record<string, string>;

  if (!shop || !code || !state) {
    return res.status(400).send("Missing params");
  }

  const savedState = req.signedCookies?.shopify_oauth_state;

  if (!savedState || savedState !== state) {
    return res.status(400).send("Invalid state");
  }

  // Clear cookie once validated
  res.clearCookie("shopify_oauth_state");

  // Exchange code → access token
  const tokenRes = await fetch(`https://${shop}/admin/oauth/access_token`, {
    method: "POST",
    headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json" },
    body: JSON.stringify({
      client_id: process.env.SHOPIFY_API_KEY,
      client_secret: process.env.SHOPIFY_API_SECRET,
      code,
    }),
  });

  if (!tokenRes.ok) {
    const text = await tokenRes.text();
    return res.status(500).send(text);
  }

  const { access_token } = await tokenRes.json();

  // TEMP response (saving to DB is next step)
  return res.send(`Installed successfully for ${shop}`);
});

export default router;

B) Replace src/routes/shopify.ts with this (full implementation)
import { Router } from "express";
import crypto from "crypto";
import payload from "payload";

const router = Router();

const SHOPIFY_API_VERSION = process.env.SHOPIFY_API_VERSION || "2025-10";
const SHOPS_COLLECTION_SLUG = "shops"; // <- change if your Payload collection slug differs

function timingSafeEqualStr(a: string, b: string) {
  const aBuf = Buffer.from(a, "utf8");
  const bBuf = Buffer.from(b, "utf8");
  if (aBuf.length !== bBuf.length) return false;
  return crypto.timingSafeEqual(aBuf, bBuf);
}

/**
 * HMAC verification for Shopify OAuth callback querystring
 * Shopify computes HMAC over sorted query params (excluding `hmac` and `signature`)
 */
function verifyOAuthHmac(query: Record<string, string | string[] | undefined>, secret: string) {
  const hmac = String(query.hmac || "");
  if (!hmac) return false;

  const message = Object.keys(query)
    .filter((k) => k !== "hmac" && k !== "signature")
    .sort()
    .map((k) => {
      const v = query[k];
      if (Array.isArray(v)) return `${k}=${v.join(",")}`;
      return `${k}=${String(v ?? "")}`;
    })
    .join("&");

  const digest = crypto.createHmac("sha256", secret).update(message).digest("hex");
  return timingSafeEqualStr(digest, hmac);
}

async function registerWebhook(params: {
  shop: string;
  accessToken: string;
  topic: string;
  address: string;
}) {
  const { shop, accessToken, topic, address } = params;

  const res = await fetch(`https://${shop}/admin/api/${SHOPIFY_API_VERSION}/webhooks.json`, {
    method: "POST",
    headers: {
      "Content-Type": "application/json",
      "X-Shopify-Access-Token": accessToken,
    },
    body: JSON.stringify({
      webhook: {
        topic,
        address,
        format: "json",
      },
    }),
  });

  if (!res.ok) {
    const text = await res.text();
    throw new Error(`Webhook create failed (${topic}): ${text}`);
  }

  return res.json();
}

/**
 * STEP 3 — START OAUTH (INSTALL)
 * GET /api/shopify/install?shop=xxx.myshopify.com
 */
router.get("/install", (req, res) => {
  const shop = String(req.query.shop || "").trim();

  if (!shop || !shop.endsWith(".myshopify.com")) {
    return res.status(400).send("Invalid shop");
  }

  const apiKey = process.env.SHOPIFY_API_KEY!;
  const appUrl = process.env.SHOPIFY_APP_URL!;
  const scopes = process.env.SHOPIFY_SCOPES!;

  if (!apiKey || !appUrl || !scopes) {
    return res.status(500).send("Missing Shopify env vars");
  }

  // CSRF state
  const state = crypto.randomBytes(16).toString("hex");

  res.cookie("shopify_oauth_state", state, {
    httpOnly: true,
    secure: true,
    sameSite: "lax",
    signed: true,
    maxAge: 10 * 60 * 1000,
  });

  const redirectUri = `${appUrl}/api/shopify/callback`;

  const installUrl =
    `https://${shop}/admin/oauth/authorize` +
    `?client_id=${encodeURIComponent(apiKey)}` +
    `&scope=${encodeURIComponent(scopes)}` +
    `&redirect_uri=${encodeURIComponent(redirectUri)}` +
    `&state=${encodeURIComponent(state)}`;

  return res.redirect(installUrl);
});

/**
 * STEP 4 — FINISH OAUTH (CALLBACK)
 * GET /api/shopify/callback?shop=...&code=...&state=...&hmac=...&timestamp=...
 */
router.get("/callback", async (req, res) => {
  try {
    const shop = String(req.query.shop || "");
    const code = String(req.query.code || "");
    const state = String(req.query.state || "");

    if (!shop || !code || !state) {
      return res.status(400).send("Missing params");
    }

    // 1) Verify cookie state
    const savedState = req.signedCookies?.shopify_oauth_state;
    if (!savedState || savedState !== state) {
      return res.status(400).send("Invalid state");
    }
    res.clearCookie("shopify_oauth_state");

    // 2) Verify Shopify HMAC
    const secret = process.env.SHOPIFY_API_SECRET!;
    if (!secret) return res.status(500).send("Missing SHOPIFY_API_SECRET");

    const okHmac = verifyOAuthHmac(req.query as any, secret);
    if (!okHmac) {
      return res.status(400).send("Invalid HMAC");
    }

    // 3) Exchange code → access token
    const tokenRes = await fetch(`https://${shop}/admin/oauth/access_token`, {
      method: "POST",
      headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json" },
      body: JSON.stringify({
        client_id: process.env.SHOPIFY_API_KEY,
        client_secret: process.env.SHOPIFY_API_SECRET,
        code,
      }),
    });

    if (!tokenRes.ok) {
      const text = await tokenRes.text();
      return res.status(500).send(text);
    }

    const tokenJson = await tokenRes.json();
    const accessToken = tokenJson.access_token as string;
    if (!accessToken) return res.status(500).send("Missing access token from Shopify");

    // 4) Upsert into Payload: shops collection
    // Fields assumed: shopDomain, accessToken, scopes, installedAt
    const scopes = process.env.SHOPIFY_SCOPES || "";
    const now = new Date().toISOString();

    const existing = await payload.find({
      collection: SHOPS_COLLECTION_SLUG,
      where: {
        shopDomain: { equals: shop },
      },
      limit: 1,
    });

    if (existing.docs?.length) {
      await payload.update({
        collection: SHOPS_COLLECTION_SLUG,
        id: existing.docs[0].id,
        data: {
          shopDomain: shop,
          accessToken, // ideally encrypt at rest (can do later)
          scopes,
          installedAt: now,
          uninstalledAt: null,
        },
      });
    } else {
      await payload.create({
        collection: SHOPS_COLLECTION_SLUG,
        data: {
          shopDomain: shop,
          accessToken,
          scopes,
          installedAt: now,
          uninstalledAt: null,
        },
      });
    }

    // 5) Register webhooks automatically
    const appUrl = process.env.SHOPIFY_APP_URL!;
    const webhookBase = `${appUrl}/api/shopify/webhooks`;

    // Use distinct endpoints per topic (recommended)
    const webhooksToCreate = [
      { topic: "orders/create", address: `${webhookBase}/orders-create` },
      { topic: "orders/updated", address: `${webhookBase}/orders-updated` },
      { topic: "products/update", address: `${webhookBase}/products-update` },
    ];

    for (const w of webhooksToCreate) {
      await registerWebhook({
        shop,
        accessToken,
        topic: w.topic,
        address: w.address,
      });
    }

    // Final redirect (to admin or UI)
    return res.redirect("/admin");
  } catch (err: any) {
    console.error(err);
    return res.status(500).send(err?.message || "Callback failed");
  }
});

export default router;
C) Add webhook receiver routes (minimal stubs)
Create a new file:

src/routes/shopify-webhooks.ts
And mount it as /api/shopify/webhooks (you already used raw body middleware for that path).

Here is a minimal pattern you can paste (this is important; Shopify will mark deliveries as failed without a 200 response):

import { Router } from "express";
import crypto from "crypto";

const router = Router();

function verifyWebhookHmac(rawBody: Buffer, hmacHeader: string, secret: string) {
  const digest = crypto.createHmac("sha256", secret).update(rawBody).digest("base64");
  const a = Buffer.from(digest, "utf8");
  const b = Buffer.from(hmacHeader || "", "utf8");
  if (a.length !== b.length) return false;
  return crypto.timingSafeEqual(a, b);
}

router.post("/orders-create", (req, res) => {
  const secret = process.env.SHOPIFY_API_SECRET!;
  const hmac = String(req.headers["x-shopify-hmac-sha256"] || "");
  const ok = verifyWebhookHmac(req.body as Buffer, hmac, secret);
  if (!ok) return res.status(401).send("Invalid webhook HMAC");

  // TODO: parse JSON and persist order
  // const payload = JSON.parse((req.body as Buffer).toString("utf8"));

  return res.status(200).send("ok");
});

router.post("/orders-updated", (req, res) => res.status(200).send("ok"));
router.post("/products-update", (req, res) => res.status(200).send("ok"));

export default router;
Then mount in your server entry:

import shopifyWebhooksRouter from "./routes/shopify-webhooks";

app.use("/api/shopify/webhooks", shopifyWebhooksRouter);

ChatGPT said:
Fly is not “changing your values” on deploy. What you are seeing in fly secrets list are digests (hash fingerprints), not the plaintext secret values.

What DIGEST means
fly secrets list never shows secret values for safety.

The DIGEST is a one-way hash of the value Fly has stored.

If the stored value is unchanged, the digest stays the same.

If you re-run fly secrets set ... with a different value (even one character), the digest changes.

So the only time Fly “changes” anything is when you (or your script/CI) set secrets again.

The most common reasons your digests keep changing
1) You are re-setting secrets from a file or script
If you run any of these repeatedly, values can change:

fly secrets import < .env

fly secrets set $(cat something)

Deploy scripts that generate random secrets (COOKIE_SECRET, PAYLOAD_SECRET, etc.)

If your .env has different values each time (or regenerated), the digest changes.

2) You accidentally included quotes / whitespace differences
These are different values to Fly:

abc vs "abc"

https://ones4app.fly.dev vs https://ones4app.fly.dev/ (trailing slash)

value vs value (extra space)
Any difference changes the digest.

3) You have duplicates / typos causing confusion
You currently have:

PAYLOAD_PUBLIC_SERVER_URL

PAYLOAD_PUBLIC_SREVER_URL (typo)

If your code reads one but you update the other, you will think Fly “changed it” when actually you are looking at two different keys.

Also you have both:

PAYLOAD_DOMAIN

SHOPIFY_APP_URL

PAYLOAD_PUBLIC_SERVER_URL

If these aren’t consistent, debugging becomes confusing.

How to confirm whether the app actually has the right values
Since Fly won’t show secrets back, you confirm by checking runtime:

Option A — SSH in and print env var names (not values)
fly ssh console -C "env | sort | findstr /i PAYLOAD"
Option B — In your app logs, print only what is safe
For example, log:

whether env vars exist

and safe-derived values like your computed serverURL (not secrets)

What you should do now (recommended cleanup)
1) Delete the typo secret key
fly secrets unset PAYLOAD_PUBLIC_SREVER_URL
2) Ensure these are correct (and consistent)
SHOPIFY_APP_URL = https://ones4app.fly.dev

PAYLOAD_PUBLIC_SERVER_URL = https://ones4app.fly.dev

PAYLOAD_DOMAIN (optional—only if your code uses it)

If you want, set them explicitly again:

fly secrets set SHOPIFY_APP_URL="https://ones4app.fly.dev" PAYLOAD_PUBLIC_SERVER_URL="https://ones4app.fly.dev"
3) Stop regenerating secrets unintentionally
If you’re using any “set secrets from .env” workflow, make sure the .env file is stable and not regenerating on each deploy.

If you tell me exactly how you are setting secrets (manual fly secrets set ..., import, CI workflow, etc.), I can point to the specific command that’s causing the digest changes.

Linux does not have findstr.

Option 2 — Show Shopify env vars

Why this matters

An “Internal Server Error” (HTTP 500) means your app crashed or threw an exception on that request. The next step is to pull the exact stack trace from Fly logs for the moment you hit the endpoint.

1) Get the real error from Fly logs (most important)
